# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-11-09

## RAG

| **[Multimodal Graph RAG for Long-range Visually Rich Document Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.28780v2)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) are widely applied to visual document understanding. However, comprehending long documents remains an issue by the limited context window. Though recent multimodal retrieval-augmented generation (MMRAG) can address this challenge by retrieving relevant pages. It still struggles with the visual question answering (VQA) requiring holistic comprehension of a document. To cope with this, knowledge graph (KG) that summarizes global knowledge of a document can provide an effective solution. However, most existing LLM-based KG construction methods handle only the language modality, leaving the automatic creation of multimodal KGs (MMKGs) for visually rich documents largely unexplored. In this paper, we introduce a multimodal graph-based RAG approach to tackle this problem. Existing LLM-based KG methods evaluate the QA performance relying on indirect evidence such as comprehensiveness, diversity, empowerment, and so on. The lack of annotated datasets for comprehensive document-level VQA poses a significant challenge to effective model evaluation. To overcome this limitation, we also introduce a new benchmark, DLVQA (document-level VQA), which provides reference summaries and corresponding supporting facts for global document-level questions. Experimental results show that our approach outperforms existing MMRAG or KG-based approaches on multi-hop QA/VQA benchmarks and DLVQA.</p></details> | WACV 2027 |
| **[HyperGraphPro: Progress-Aware Reinforcement Learning for Structure-Guided Hypergraph RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.17755v3)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-hop question answering requires retrieval agents to construct a connected evidence path across reasoning turns rather than retrieve isolated relevant evidence for a single query. However, in existing GraphRAG methods, retrieval and reinforcement learning are often optimized with mismatched signals: retrieval is driven by local semantic relevance, while outcome-level reinforcement learning assigns the reward by assessing the final answer without identifying which retrieval actions progress the reasoning path. We introduce HyperGraphPro, a progress-aware hypergraph RAG framework that focuses on evidence-path progress as the shared objective for retrieval and policy optimization. For retrieval, HyperGraphPro reranks candidate hyperedges using query-conditioned structural distinctiveness, combining entity-query relevance with a local-vs-global hypergraph incidence signal to favor entities that are informative in the current graph neighborhood. For policy optimization, HyperGraphPro augments uniform trajectory-level reward assignment with stepwise progress-aware policy optimization, which assigns step-level rewards according to the coverage of answer-guided target hyperedges. Experiments on multi-hop question answering benchmarks demonstrate that HyperGraphPro consistently improves reasoning accuracy over existing GraphRAG methods.</p></details> | In progress |
| **[Beyond Visual Boundaries: Rethinking Scene Segmentation for Movie RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28699v2)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Understanding long-form video remains a fundamental challenge for multimodal large language models (MLLMs). Sparse frame sampling fails to capture fine-grained visual details, while dense sampling quickly exceeds context length limits. Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) offers a promising middle ground by selectively retrieving relevant video segments for grounded generation, yet its effectiveness critically depends on the quality of the video segments used as retrieval units. In this paper, we investigate RAG for movie understanding, which demands story-level reasoning over characters, events, and narrative arcs spanning hours of content. Scene segmentation, a long-studied problem that partitions movies into semantically coherent units, is a natural candidate for defining such retrieval units. We reexamine whether existing methods actually serve this role through comprehensive evaluation on downstream movie understanding tasks, and find that they do not provide a consistent or dominant advantage over naive uniform temporal chunking. Our audit of the most standard scene segmentation benchmarks reveals why: current annotations prioritize visually salient transitions over narrative event structure. Motivated by this mismatch, we introduce NarraScene, a narrative-centric scene segmentation dataset annotated with a three-level cognitive taxonomy spanning physical, character, and narrative change, where every valid boundary requires a narrative-level shift. When used as retrieval units, these narrative-grounded segments outperform uniform chunking on downstream movie understanding tasks, suggesting that the central challenge for scene segmentation in movie RAG is not detecting boundaries, but identifying the narrative event units that matter for movie understanding. The dataset is available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/queez0405/NarraScene.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to NeurIPS 2026 (Evaluations & Datasets Track)</p></details> |
| **[Embodied RAG++: Retrieval-Augmented Generation From What Exists to What Works in Embodied Scenes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07517v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>To serve a request in a human environment, a robot system must find an object that satisfies a functional requirement and a robot that can feasibly reach it. Scene graphs record which objects exist and where, but they often omit capabilities documented only in product manuals. Retrieval-augmented generation methods can read such manuals, yet they rarely reason jointly over object capabilities, physical instances, robot states, and dynamic connectivity. We introduce E2W-Bench, the Exists-to-Works Benchmark, which links dynamic hybrid scene graphs with real product manuals to evaluate capability-grounded robot--object assignment under availability, mobility, vertical-reach, and connector constraints. We also present Embodied RAG++, a spatially grounded retrieval framework that scopes retrieval to the manuals linked to candidate objects, caches extracted capabilities per product model, and resolves robot--object feasibility through deterministic spatial reasoning. On E2W-Bench, Embodied RAG++ reaches a pair accuracy of 0.90 with Qwen2.5-32B and 1.00 with Llama3.3-70B, compared with at most 0.30 and 0.55 for the baselines, while requiring the shortest preprocessing time among the retrieval-based methods and tying for the lowest total token count.</p></details> | accepted to SII 2027 |
| **[Trustworthy RAG: An Evaluation Agent for Detecting Misinformation and Knowledge Poisoning in Generative AI Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21095v2)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) grounds Large Language Model (LLM) outputs in external knowledge, but RAG systems usually trust whatever they retrieve, creating a Security-Reliability Gap: high semantic relevance does not guarantee factual truth. Adversaries exploit this through knowledge poisoning, inserting malicious documents to cause targeted misinformation. We propose an Evaluation Agent, middleware that combines Natural Language Inference (NLI) factual verification, a five-signal poison detector with relevance-weighted aggregation, and a Trust Index T = 0.4 F + 0.35 C + 0.25 (1 - P ) with a non-linear dampener for high-contamination contexts. On TruthfulQA with Llama 3.3 70B, the agent reaches 91% accuracy and 100% precision, with 100% recall on instruction injection, while in-place edits, such as entity swaps, remain hard to detect. Across three LLMs the Trust Index stays discriminative, with a Receiver Operating Characteristic Area Under the Curve (ROC-AUC) of 0.73 to 0.81; generation style matters more than model size, and per-LLM threshold calibration restores baseline competitive accuracy, whereas a weaker FEVER result shows that cross-dataset generalization requires domain-specific calibration. In a software-engineering use case, a secure-coding assistant over guidance from the Open Worldwide Application Security Project (OWASP) Top 10 and the Common Weakness Enumeration (CWE), the agent reliably blocks instruction injection of unsafe advice (F1 92%), while contradiction and subtle semantic weakening remain hard. Throughout, the agent measures detection of poisoned context before generation, not whether the LLM adopts the injected misinformation. We release the proposed approach, attack generator, and experimental artifacts at the link: https://github.com/GPT-Laboratory/TrustworthyRAG.</p></details> | <details><summary>7 pag...</summary><p>7 pages, 1 figure, 6 tables. Published in the Main Research Track of the Twenty-First International Conference on Software Engineering Advances (ICSEA 2026)</p></details> |
| **[Reliability Stress Testing of RAG Retrieval under Query-Relevant Corpus Poisoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04264v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>External text can affect a retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) system only after the retriever admits it to the model context. We stress this admission step while varying document content, attacker query information, per-target poison budget, and contamination scope. Across 623 Benchmarking Information Retrieval (BEIR) queries from SciFact and NFCorpus, five query-targeted documents per query reduce classical-retriever nDCG@10 by 40.2-61.5% in isolated-target stress. Full-query anchoring explains most Best Matching 25 (BM25) degradation, whereas appended text also shifts latent semantic analysis (LSA) and Hybrid rankings. A pretrained BGE-small validation reaches 100% target exposure for query-only P=5 on both datasets; adding the injection payload changes SciFact nDCG only from 0.269 to 0.271 and lowers NFCorpus exposure slightly to 98.8%, indicating that query anchoring remains the dominant retrieval-side factor for this dense model. Query length materially changes the interpretation of partial-information attacks: on 162 long SciFact queries, two random terms cover 19.9% of eligible terms and still yield 39.5% Hybrid target exposure. Multi-target contamination adds cross-target interference. Overlap-based admission can remove full-query poisons but may sharply reduce clean retrieval utility, and maximal marginal relevance (MMR) leaves exposure unchanged under the tested configuration. The study measures retrieval-layer failures, not downstream instruction following.</p></details> |  |
| **[Revisiting RAG Retrievers: An Information Theoretic Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.21553v2)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems rely critically on the retriever module to surface relevant context for large language models. Although numerous retrievers have recently been proposed, each built on different ranking principles such as lexical matching, dense embeddings, or graph citations, there remains a lack of systematic understanding of how these mechanisms differ and overlap. Existing benchmarks primarily compare entire RAG pipelines or rely on aggregate ranking metrics (Recall, MRR, nDCG) that assume item-level independence, providing little guidance on redundancy or complementarity between retrievers. This work presents MIGRASCOPE, a Mutual Information based RAG Retriever Analysis Scope. We introduce principled metrics grounded in information theory to quantify retrieval quality, redundancy/synergy, and marginal contribution. Although the framework is designed to be applicable to any setting where a ground-truth answer can construct a pseudo chunk distribution, the present empirical study focuses on multi-hop question answering - a demanding setting where retriever complementarity is especially pronounced. On four corpora and thirteen SOTA retrievers spanning lexical, dense, and graph-based families, we show that (i) the MI-based divergence metric captures retriever quality dimensions not reflected by Recall or MRR, (ii) graph-based retrievers are redundant with each other while BM25 remains informationally complementary, and (iii) in the multi-hop QA setting, a carefully chosen ensemble guided by MI-based attribution can outperform any single retriever. We position these findings as task-specific insights and provide a reusable framework for retriever analysis that practitioners can re-estimate on their own target domains.</p></details> |  |
| **[IAMRAGBENCH: Benchmarking Authorization Expansions in LLM-Generated AWS IAM Policies under RAG Poisoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03431v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) using retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) can synthesize Amazon Web Services (AWS) Identity and Access Management (IAM) policies from tasks and operational documents. Attacker-controlled context may induce provider-valid policies that exceed trusted authorization requirements. We present IAMRAGBENCH, a 60-case benchmark spanning scope, conditions, explicit denies, trust, permissions boundaries, and role delegation. It combines deterministic authorization-envelope scoring with case-specific validation, including request-level checks where supported. An attack requires a provider-valid clean policy that satisfies the task and a provider-valid treatment policy that violates the envelope, matches a predeclared goal, preserves an intended request, and newly authorizes a forbidden request. Across five endpoints, included-context exposure produces 49 exact authorization deltas, including 46 goal-matched attacks (46/292; 15.75\%). End-to-end BM25 over 720 documents retrieves the designated poison for all 60 tasks at \(k=5\) but produces 7/294 goal-matched attacks (2.38\%). Presenting the same weakening as a final security architecture exception produces 74/116 attacks in a frozen two-endpoint stress test (63.79\%) and 115/294 in a post hoc all-five extension using the same frozen prompts (39.12\%). In a replay of 194 provider-valid treatment policies that violate task requirements, AWS \texttt{CheckNoNewAccess} reports no new access for four of 168 goal-matched exact attacks. The full pre-deployment rule rejects all 171 exact deltas and routes five unresolved cases to review. These findings support treating IAM copilots as untrusted proposal generators whose outputs require independent authorization checks before deployment.</p></details> | <details><summary>20 pa...</summary><p>20 pages, 4 figures, 5 tables</p></details> |
| **[Bridging Static and Agentic RAG for Taiwanese Historical Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23056v2)** | 2026-11-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) enables language models to adapt retrieval based on previously retrieved evidence, but it remains unclear whether such adaptive orchestration consistently outperforms well-designed static pipelines. We conduct a controlled comparison of agentic and static RAG for Taiwanese historical question answering, sharing the same generator and hybrid retrieval backend. Despite similar aggregate performance, the two pipelines differ on 70.83% of questions, with their advantages largely canceling out when averaged. An oracle that selects the better response per question improves the composite score by 0.2417 over the better individual pipeline, revealing substantial headroom for question-level selection. We therefore introduce a post-hoc selector that compares the two responses and their cited evidence, significantly outperforming either individual pipeline and recovering 60.34% of the oracle headroom. These results show that aggregate comparisons can obscure meaningful question-level differences between retrieval strategies, suggesting that exploiting their complementarity may be more fruitful than seeking a universally superior pipeline.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by TAAI 2026</p></details> |
| **[RAG-TRACE: Tracing Measurement Evidence and Diagnostic Completeness in AI-Generated ECG Reports](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01409v1)** | 2026-11-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Purpose: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) can introduce electrocardiogram (ECG) criteria that lack patient-specific measurement support. This study evaluates the reliability of retrieved diagnostic criteria in AI-generated ECG reports. Methods: We analyzed 1,083 human-validated PTB-XL patient records using a library of 47 criteria cards, including 33 without numeric verification rules. Language models generated structured reports with and without retrieval. Criterion applicability was evaluated using Marquette 12SL measurements, with independent assessment using Uni-G and comparison against expert-validated diagnostic labels. Results: Keyword retrieval introduced untestable or measurement-disconfirmed criteria in 46.8% of GPT-4.1-mini reports. A deterministic verification rule withdrew 647 criterion applications, including 639 without numeric checks, while preserving 268 of 269 applications independently supported by Uni-G. Restricting retrieval to 14 testable criteria reduced the initial unsupported-criterion rate to 13.8%. Under matched instructions and an identical output schema, retrieval increased unsupported-criterion reports from 17.4% to 31.1%, while hypertrophy recall remained unchanged at 62.6%. Conclusion: These findings identify a fundamental limitation of retrieval-based clinical report generation: retrieved criteria do not necessarily provide valid patient-specific evidence. Measurement-based verification can reduce unsupported criterion applications, but diagnostic validity requires independent assessment.</p></details> | <details><summary>Code ...</summary><p>Code and study materials available at https://github.com/Elyasirankhah/RAG-TRACE</p></details> |
| **[DeFT-RAG: Decomposing Multi-Hop Questions into Self-Contained Sub-Questions with Reusable Answers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37826v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) lets a large language model (LLM) answer questions by retrieving relevant passages from a large corpus. However, RAG struggles with multi-hop questions, which require chaining facts spread across several documents through intermediate entities that the question never mentions. Graph-based RAG methods retrieve once from the entities a question names and so rarely recover the full evidence chain. Iterative RAG methods recover it step by step, but their accuracy is limited by the many tasks they ask the LLM to perform, such as planning the sub-questions, deciding their dependencies, rewriting them as answers arrive, and keeping track of what is known. We propose DeFT-RAG, a RAG framework built on three components. First, DeFT-RAG generates one self-contained sub-question at a time from the original question and the sub-questions answered so far with their answers (QA pairs). Each sub-question names every entity it asks about, which removes the need to plan, track dependencies or rewrite, and lets the LLM focus on a single fine-grained task at a time. Second, DeFT-RAG moves the remaining LLM work outside the LLM: rule-based updates to a QA table that keeps track of what is known, and yes/no decisions made by a fine-tuned lightweight decision model. Third, DeFT-RAG caches the QA pairs and reuses an answer when a later question asks a sub-question with the same meaning. On MuSiQue, 2WikiMultiHopQA and HotpotQA, DeFT-RAG achieves the best accuracy among thirteen baselines with both gpt-4o-mini and Qwen3.6 as the backbone, while giving the LLM fewer input tokens than any other iterative method. With a QA cache shared across 1,000 MuSiQue questions, 23.1% of sub-questions are answered from the cache, which reduces LLM calls by 12.9% without lowering accuracy.</p></details> | <details><summary>12 pa...</summary><p>12 pages, 2 figures, 7 tables. Code: https://github.com/Dingyi-Kang/DeFT-RAG</p></details> |
| **[Logic Hijacking of RAG: Single-Document Poisoning via Local Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34829v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Single-document corpus poisoning under black-box access poses a severe threat to Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG). However, existing attacks predominantly rely on explicit factual refutations or imperative instruction overrides. Modern aligned LLMs often arbitrate such conflicts conservatively, rejecting overt contradictions in favor of parametric memory or clean retrieved context while triggering input guardrails. We propose MONOLOGUE, a single-document poisoning framework based on Logic Hijacking without explicit refutation. Rather than disputing the ground truth, our method concedes it as an authoritative historical baseline before introducing the update, weaving a grounded record-correction narrative (e.g., dated institutional reattributions) that presents the counterfactual target as the most recent update. To prevent suspicious live writes, payloads are refined entirely offline via local multi-dimensional gated optimization, coupling automated text quality audits with surrogate behavioral verification. Evaluations across three benchmarks, three retrieval backends, and four open-weight generator regimes show that our method achieves the strongest overall attack efficacy among the evaluated single-document poisoning attacks.</p></details> | Updated affiliations |
| **[Pix2Evidence: A Multimodal RAG System for Text and Visual Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41335v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) over visually rich documents must find both relevant content and the evidence that supports an answer. Screenshot retrieval preserves images and page structure, but written facts within screenshots also need to be searchable. Moreover, topical relevance alone does not identify which retrieved tile contains evidence for the answer. We introduce Pix2Evidence, a multimodal RAG system that encodes each screenshot tile together with its available transcription into a single fused image--text representation, so that layout and readable text are considered jointly during retrieval. To guide evidence selection, a vision-language teacher describes the visible content that supports each training question. We use these descriptions to adapt the retriever, then rerank the retrieved candidates before answer generation. Extensive experiments demonstrate that Pix2Evidence outperforms state-of-the-art methods. Across five widely-used Wikipedia QA benchmarks, the non-reranked Pix2Evidence leads the main comparison in answer accuracy on four. On MMSearch, it reaches 26.0\%, 4.4 percentage points above PixelRAG. We will release the code and share derived data where licensing and privacy reviews permit.</p></details> |  |
| **[Language Models Show Conformity Bias in RAG: When the Majority Endorses a Poisoned Claim](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41012v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) can expose language models to misleading evidence in retrieved documents. We investigate whether increasing the number of mutually agreeing poisoned documents affects a model's ability to detect misinformation. We evaluate 200 randomly selected questions from SciFact (random seed 38), comparing contexts with one poisoned and six healthy documents against contexts with six mutually agreeing poisoned documents and one healthy document. For Gemma-3-12b, poison-detection rates decrease from 25% (50/200) in the minority condition to 10% (20/200) in the majority condition. Among the 38 questions with different outcomes across conditions, 34 change from detected to undetected, while only four change in the opposite direction (exact McNemar test, p = 6.04e-7). This pattern is consistent with a conformity-like response to a majority of mutually agreeing poisoned documents. Our results provide initial evidence of conformity bias in RAG-based misinformation detection: Gemma-3-12b is substantially less likely to detect poisoned claims when they dominate the retrieved evidence.</p></details> |  |
| **[SoK: Agentic Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG): Taxonomy, Architectures, Evaluation, and Research Directions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.07379v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems are increasingly evolving into agentic architectures where large language models autonomously coordinate multi-step reasoning, dynamic memory management, and iterative retrieval strategies. Despite rapid industrial adoption, current research lacks a systematic understanding of Agentic RAG as a sequential decision-making system, leading to highly fragmented architectures, inconsistent evaluation methodologies, and unresolved reliability risks. This Systematization of Knowledge (SoK) paper provides the first unified framework for understanding these autonomous systems. We formalize agentic retrieval-generation loops as finite-horizon partially observable Markov decision processes, explicitly modeling their control policies and state transitions. Building upon this formalization, we develop a comprehensive taxonomy and modular architectural decomposition that categorizes systems by their planning mechanisms, retrieval orchestration, memory paradigms, and tool-invocation behaviors. We further analyze the critical limitations of traditional static evaluation practices and identify severe systemic risks inherent to autonomous loops, including compounding hallucination propagation, memory poisoning, retrieval misalignment, and cascading tool-execution vulnerabilities. Finally, we outline key doctoral-scale research directions spanning stable adaptive retrieval, cost-aware orchestration, formal trajectory evaluation, and oversight mechanisms, providing a definitive roadmap for building reliable, controllable, and scalable agentic retrieval systems.</p></details> | <details><summary>arXiv...</summary><p>arXiv admin note: This submission has been withdrawn because it does not meet arXiv's research content quality standards</p></details> |
| **[Better Retrievers, Worse Labels: How Incomplete Relevance Judgments Distort Abstention Evaluation in Conversational RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40966v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Conversational RAG benchmarks such as MTRAG and RETECO evaluate retrieval and generation end to end: each system answers from its own retrieval, so abstention must be scored against whether each retrieved context is sufficient. A convenient shortcut reads sufficiency off the relevance judgments and treats a context without gold documents as unanswerable. We re-run the official baselines exactly and test this shortcut on RECOR (RETECO track~2) with four retrievers (nDCG@10 from .18 to .59) and three Qwen3 readers. Two open LLM judges and a panel of four small open models label each context by content, and a model of another family audits 250 contexts blind, with adjudication. The stronger the retriever, the larger the share of no-gold contexts that still contain the answer (28\% to 72\%) and the lower the shortcut's precision (.68 to .10; .70 to .21 under the audit). A reader agrees: Qwen3-4B answers 62--67\% of these false misses correctly, against 23--30\% of no-gold contexts judged insufficient and 1\% with no documents. In most false misses, the answer comes from a hard negative mined for the same question, another turn's gold or a neighbouring chunk of the gold page. On MTRAG, whose judgments come from annotators, precision drops from .72 to .50. Under the shortcut, answerability AUROC is up to .19 too low, and the best sufficiency detector appears to be a retrieval score that never reads the documents. We release code, runs and labels, and recommend scoring refusals against content labels, not the presence of gold.</p></details> |  |
| **[Decompose, Estimate, Select: Coverage-Aware Context Construction for Long-Form RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40314v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) grounds a language model's output in retrieved evidence, but for complex questions spanning multiple aspects of an information need, relevance-based retrieval may return redundant evidence while leaving some aspects unsupported. We propose a coverage-aware retrieval pipeline that decomposes queries into subquestions, ranks candidates with a coverage-trained bi-encoder, predicts a document\,$\times$\,subquestion coverage matrix with a cross-encoder, and selects documents by greedy maximum-marginal coverage. On TREC RAG 2024, our approach improves $α$-nDCG@10, which rewards newly covered nuggets and discounts redundant evidence, from $0.625$ to $0.702$, approaching the $0.718$ gold-nugget upper bound without sacrificing relevance. We then find that ground-truth oracle retrieval, despite substantially higher retrieval coverage, delivers no more nugget coverage to the answer than our estimated selection. This suggests that answer completeness depends not only on what evidence is retrieved, but also on how effectively the generator uses it. To address this, we introduce a \emph{grounded self-feedback loop} in which the model identifies underused evidence from its first answer and generates again without gold supervision, raising $V_{\mathrm{strict}}$, the fraction of vital reference nuggets fully supported by the answer, by $0.086$ over two passes while largely preserving faithfulness.</p></details> |  |
| **[Prevented is Not Protected: What Attack Success Rate Hides about RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40742v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is vulnerable to knowledge poisoning, where adversaries manipulate retrieved evidence to influence model outputs. Existing defenses are primarily evaluated using attack success rate (ASR), which obscures defense-induced failures and the mechanisms behind apparent attack prevention. To address this issue, this work introduces \textbf{Aligned RAG Evaluation}, a matched-query protocol that jointly examines clean and poisoned responses, with and without defense. By combining matched conditions analysis, LLM-judge endorsement, and retrieval dependence controls, the proposed framework distinguishes prevented from introduced failures, target mentions from LLM endorsement, and single-passage influence from evidence composition. Experiments on HotpotQA and Natural Questions reveal that existing defenses can introduce unexpected target-positive answers, although they can prevent some attacks, while evaluation criteria can also change defense rankings. Our findings expose critical blind spots in ASR-based evaluation and motivate a more comprehensive assessment of RAG poisoning defenses. The code and the evaluation artifact are available at https://github.com/chxhana/aligned-rag-eval.</p></details> |  |
| **[TCC-RAG: Temporal Causal Chain-Guided RAG for Temporal Knowledge Graph Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38366v1)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have spurred growing interest in temporal knowledge graph question answering (TKGQA). Most existing methods rely on semantic retrieval augmented generation (RAG), which suffers from fundamental limitations in complex temporal reasoning. On the one hand, semantic-based RAG methods struggle to capture the multi-hop causal knowledge required for complex temporal reasoning. On the other hand, these methods introduce excessive redundant knowledge, trapping LLMs in spurious reasoning chains that potentially result in erroneous answers. To address these problems, we propose a Temporal Causal Chain-guided RAG (TCC-RAG) to retrieve a concise yet sufficient set of causal knowledge to support complex temporal reasoning. Specifically, we reformulate the temporal knowledge graph into a multi-view causal cue graph, with causal cues inducing connections that explicitly encode temporal evolution and causal dynamics. Based on the causal cue graph, we construct a query-specific temporal causal chain to guide the retrieval process, enabling the model to focus on a concise set of causally relevant knowledge. Finally, we introduce a causal evidence filter to refine the retrieved knowledge, ensuring its causal consistency with the underlying temporal logic of the query. Experiments show that TCC-RAG achieves substantial performance gains of 10.72% and 8.40% on two TKGQA benchmarks, and delivers a remarkable 62.97% improvement for multi-step temporal reasoning.</p></details> | <details><summary>14 pa...</summary><p>14 pages, 6 figures, ICDE2027 Research Track</p></details> |
| **[When Is Additional Retrieval Worthwhile? A Cost-Aware Study of Evidence Gain and Answer Reliability in RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37358v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) can benefit from more sophisticated retrieval, but extra retrieval may be unnecessary for many questions and gains in evidence retrieval may not translate into faithful or correct answers. We study a cost-aware, two-action retrieval policy that chooses between a dense-retrieval-plus-reranking baseline and a hybrid lexical--dense retriever. Rather than classifying baseline retrieval as simply successful or unsuccessful, we estimate the question-specific incremental evidence-retrieval benefit of hybrid retrieval using features available before the routing decision. A regularized linear model and decision threshold are selected using document-grouped cross-validation and frozen prior to held-out evaluation. On 60 previously unseen QASPER training-split articles (188 questions, 169 evidence-evaluable), the policy triggers hybrid retrieval on 46 questions (24.5\%) and obtains the same evidence Hit@10 as always-hybrid retrieval (78.70\%, versus 76.92\% for always-keep). Paired, paper-cluster bootstrap intervals do not establish a reliable utility advantage. We further compare generated answers from a 4-bit Qwen3-4B model under the three retrieval policies. Provisional answer-level assessments show no clear correctness or faithfulness improvement after partial review, despite the evidence-retrieval result. The generation assessments remain model-assisted and lack independent human validation. These results expose an important distinction between retrieving relevant evidence efficiently and improving the reliability of the generated answer. The work is presented as a reproducible empirical study, not a claim of statistically established answer-quality superiority.</p></details> |  |
| **[p-DCIM: A 28nm Probabilistic SRAM DCIM based Ising Machine for Near-Memory Context Selection in LLM-RAG Pipeline](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37033v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present a fabricated SRAM DCIM Ising machine that performs set-level context selection for large language model (LLM) with retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). In RAG, the evidence-selection stage is increasingly delegated to repeated language-model reranking, which adds substantial energy to the inference pipeline. We reformulate evidence selection as a quadratic unconstrained binary optimization (QUBO) problem and solve it on a dual-mode digital compute-in-memory (DCIM) macro: the same multiply-and-accumulate datapath computes the embedding-similarity scores that populate the QUBO coefficients and evaluates the per-spin Hamiltonian differences that drive the anneal. We test this across four instruction-tuned models on HotpotQA and SQuAD. After matching faithful and hallucinated answers within word-count bins, length-only classifiers fall near chance, while perplexity reaches 0.81--0.91 AUROC on targeted unsupported edits. Varying retrieval depth shows why the score changes meaning: detection can weaken with only the top-ranked passage, then recover after adding a second passage. A combined uncertainty and semantic-drift score improves calibration, and measurements remain above chance on RAGTruth QA. Results support a conditional view of uncertainty as a useful measure of RAG hallucination risk.</p></details> | <details><summary>6 pag...</summary><p>6 pages. Accepted for oral presentation at ICNLSP 2026</p></details> |
| **[RAG-RST: Retrieved Exemplars as In-Context Discourse Schema for RST Parsing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22150v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In Rhetorical Structure Theory (RST), each edge of a discourse tree carries a nuclearity label, marking which span holds the central content, and a relation drawn from a closed inventory of functional categories. Predicting these labels therefore requires a formal discourse schema, and exposure to discourse-rich pretraining text does not supply it: given only a target EDU pair, a strong LLM recovers nuclearity at 0.794 F1 and relation at 0.738 F1. We propose RAG-RST, a retrieval-augmented framework that supplies this schema as in-context demonstration rather than through parameter updates, raising nuclearity to 0.846 F1 and relation to 0.788 F1 with no training. Two configurations are compared: a precomputed strategy that labels all EDU pairs before parsing, and a dynamic strategy that retrieves at each reduce step, so that the exemplars reaching the generator depend on the reductions the parser has already committed to. Both outperform existing RST parsers across span, nuclearity, relation, and full tree metrics, with the dynamic variant consistently strongest. Two properties of the retrieved schema emerge: its utility is bounded in number, saturating and then declining beyond fifty exemplars, and it depends on structural position. | **[Cost-Efficient RAG for Entity Matching with LLMs: A Blocking-based Batch Processing Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.05708v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) offer a compelling approach to entity matching (EM) in label-scarce settings, yet their matching quality is sensitive to records with ambiguous or sparse attributes due to the absence of external factual grounding. To address the above limitation, we introduce a CE-RAG4EM (Cost-Efficient RAG for Entity Matching) framework that reduces overhead through blocking-based batch retrieval and generation. We investigate the benefits of this framework through a systematic analysis of RAG systems for entity matching, focusing on blocking-aware batch optimizations and retrieval granularity. Extensive experiments on nine widely used real-world entity matching benchmark datasets show that CE-RAG4EM under a zero-shot setting achieves comparable or improved matching quality while substantially reducing end-to-end overhead. The results demonstrate that our CE-RAG4EM closes the knowledge grounding gap of the LLM-EM baseline and yields significant improvements in F1 scores on datasets with ambiguous or numerically sparse attributes, while its batch retrieval and inference substantially reduce the overhead and maintain competitive matching quality compared to naive RAG4EM. Our analysis identifies clear performance-overhead trade-offs across blocking-based batch sizes and retrieval granularity, showing how matching quality and computational cost shift with each design choice. These findings offer concrete guidance for building scalable, efficient RAG systems for entity matching that balance accuracy and cost.</p></details> | <details><summary>Exten...</summary><p>Extended version of the paper accepted at ACM SIGMOD 2027</p></details> |
| **[Poisoning the Neighborhood: A Topology-Aware Topic-Level Attack on Large-Scale HNSW-based RAG Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21464v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) mitigates hallucinations and knowledge staleness in large language models using external knowledge. We organize poisoned documents into a Hub-Satellite structure: Satellites provide competitive entry points across semantic regions, while Hubs strengthen local cohesion and facilitate cascading retrieval. For generation, we propose an entity-claim promotion attack combining evidence anchoring and entity-claim coupling to promote the target entity via a target claim across different query regions. On 2M-document Wikipedia, our attack maintains over 72% retrieval success and reaches 31.29% attack success, substantially outperforming baselines; domain-specific experiments further support its effectiveness. Structural analyses show that the Hub-Satellite topology forms in HNSW, indicating that graph structure contributes to improved poisoned-document retrieval. For Loud, increasing either the visual token budget or visual fidelity, the image resolution those tokens preserve, improves accuracy with the other held fixed, and full-page scaling outperforms repetition and tiling at matched budgets. For Clear, suppressing surrounding content improves accuracy without enlarging selected regions, and adding OCR of these regions to page images outperforms either input alone. We implement both as Loud and Clear (LoC), which combines higher-resolution pages with OCR from query-relevant regions without ground-truth annotations or additional training. On 1,000 MuSiQue questions, the number of wrong answers with unretrieved required evidence falls from 382 to 234 with all-MiniLM-L6-v2 and from 383 to 274 with Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B. These counts correspond to 69% to 44% and 66% to 48% of each system's wrong answers, respectively. Crucially, evidence recovery does not produce a proportional reduction in total error: premature resolutions and abstentions increase as missing-evidence failures decrease. | **[When More Documents Hurt RAG: Measuring and Reducing Vector Search Dilution with Domain-Scoped Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11350v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) degrades when applied to large, heterogeneous document collections. Even under hybrid dense+sparse retrieval, we first observed it in a deployed Wyoming Department of Transportation (WYDOT) system; in a controlled experiment, growing the corpus from 54 to 1,128 documents cut precision@10 by 63% and recall of the answer-bearing document by 23%. In MASDR-RAG, domain scoping is the load-bearing component. We found hard partitioning to be unsafe, whereas training-free soft scoping is safe when the scope structure is real. Furthermore, we can discover these scopes without hand-curated metadata. Based on these varied outcomes, our practical recommendation is simple: soft-scope first, then perform a single synthesis call.</p></details> | <details><summary>19 pa...</summary><p>19 pages, 5 figures, 20 tables. | **[Copying Is Not Grounding: Training Faithful Long-Form RAG with Authored-Sentence Credit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18442v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In search engines and assistants, long-form RAG answers can mislead readers with claims their sources do not support. Faithfulness rewards, the share of supported sentences, train models toward fewer such claims. We show that these rewards are hacked by copying. A sentence copied from the passages is supported almost by construction, so trained models learn to copy rather than ground what they write. Common defenses only move the hack: a KL or copy penalty leaves copying in place or trades it for shorter, emptier answers and refusals. GRPO, the standard RL algorithm, makes it worse by giving every sentence its answer's advantage, reinforcing copied and grounded sentences alike. We propose Authored-Sentence Credit (ASC) , which fixes both by moving GRPO's reward and advantage from the answer to the sentence level. The reward credits only sentences that are authored rather than copied, say more than the question, and are supported by the passages. The advantage compares each sentence with every sentence sampled for the question, so one that earns no credit ranks last and is never reinforced. Across four models and two in-domain, three held-out corpora, only ASC lowers the error rate of authored sentences and writes more supported ones in every setting, by 38% and 46% at the median under held-out evaluators, giving readers more grounded information. It takes none of these shortcuts, and two LLM judges comparing whole answers prefer no baseline to the base model but rate ASC's answers on par with it. We conclude that a faithfulness reward should credit what a model writes, not what it copies.</p></details> |  |
| **[PerCache: Predictive Hierarchical Cache for RAG Applications on Mobile Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.11553v2)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) has become a de facto paradigm in various large language model (LLM)-driven applications on mobile devices, such as mobile assistants leveraging personal emails or meeting records. To address this issue, one promising approach is to reuse intermediate computational results across different queries to eliminate redundant computation. But most existing approaches, such as key-value (KV) cache reuse and semantic cache reuse, are designed for cloud settings and perform poorly on mobile devices as they overlook the distinctive characteristics of mobile RAG. We propose PerCache, a novel hierarchical cache framework designed for reducing end-to-end latency of personalized RAG applications on mobile platforms. PerCache adopts a hierarchical architecture that progressively matches similar queries and query-key-value (QKV) cache to maximize the reuse of intermediate results at different computing stages. To improve cache hit rate, PerCache applies a predictive method to populate the cache with queries that are likely to be raised in the future. In addition, PerCache can adapt its configurations to dynamic system loads, aiming to maximize caching utility with constrained resource consumption. We implement PerCache on top of an existing mobile LLM inference engine and deploy it on commodity mobile phones. Extensive evaluations show that PerCache achieves the lowest average end-to-end latency across five datasets and two LLM architectures, reducing latency by up to 63.5% over the strongest baseline while maintaining comparable answer quality.</p></details> |  |

| **[EvoRAG: Making Knowledge Graph-based RAG Automatically Evolve through Feedback Backpropagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.15676v2)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Knowledge Graph-based Retrieval-Augmented Generation (KG-RAG) has emerged as a promising paradigm for enhancing LLM reasoning by retrieving multi-hop paths from KGs. These frameworks struggle to adapt to task-specific requirements and lack mechanisms to filter low-contribution knowledge during generation. However, a key challenge lies in effectively linking response-level feedback to triplet-level contribution evaluation and knowledge updates in the KG. In this work, we propose EvoRAG, a self-evolving KG-RAG framework that leverages the feedback over generated responses to continuously refine the KG and enhance reasoning accuracy. EvoRAG introduces a feedback-driven backpropagation mechanism that attributes feedback to retrieved paths by measuring their utility for response and propagates this utility back to individual triplets, supporting fine-grained KG refinements towards more adaptive and accurate reasoning. Through EvoRAG, we establish a closed loop that couples feedback, LLM, and graph data, continuously enhancing the performance and robustness in real-world scenarios. Experimental results show that EvoRAG improves reasoning accuracy by $7.34\%$ over state-of-the-art KG-RAG frameworks. | **[Fast and Accurate: Improving Personalized LLM Performance by Semantic Concentration Inspired Workload Split between RAG and Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15921v1)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>With ever increasing personalized large language model (LLM) services, the next question to answer is how to improve the quality of services/users' experiences, which is indicated by personalized LLM generation accuracy/quality and inference latency. During the online LLM inference, PUR directly generates responses for ``core'' queries covered by the PEFT region and invokes RAG only for sporadic ``peripheral'' queries. Its tree-structured activity ontology decomposes classification into local decisions among semantically related activities. The static store supplies node-constrained examples, while offline-discovered executable signal features provide numerical evidence for local distinctions. During inference, the personalized store collects selected user observations, which the LLM integrates with static knowledge and signal features while traversing the ontology to an activity label. | **[Training-Free BioNEL-RAG: Advancing Biomedical Entity Linking through Retrieval-Augmented Generation and Large Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14789v1)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Biomedical Named Entity Linking (BioNEL) is the task of mapping mentions extracted from biomedical texts to canonical entities or concepts in the standardized knowledge base. | **[Frustratingly Easy Agentic RAG via Portable Skills: A Case Study in Customer Support](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14428v1)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Enterprise workflow automation typically requires retrieving a complete and accurate set of relevant information from large operational document corpora (SOPs, policy documents) given a specific scenario or request. Our claim is scoped to that setting: a mature, well-curated corpus, a latency-tolerant workflow, and human review of every case. | **[ORCAGen: Orchestrating Context-Aware Malware Deception with RAG-Guided Generative AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12415v1)** | 2026-10-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Malware defenses often remove or isolate suspicious programs as quickly as possible. ORCAGen combines Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) with structured prompt engineering to generate both proof-of-concept (PoC) malware and corresponding deception orchestration code. A curated knowledge base (KB) of malware procedures and active defense strategies grounds the generation process, helping the LLM produce threat-specific and executable deception logic rather than generic or hallucinated outputs. The generated PoC malware provides a safe and reproducible way to test whether a deception strategy can disrupt, redirect, or suppress targeted malware behavior before the strategy is added to the runtime playbook. We evaluate ORCAGen across GPT-4o, GPT-5.5, Gemini 3.5 Flash, Qwen3-Coder, and Claude Sonnet 4.5 using execution success, hallucination rate, refinement effort, deception effectiveness, and runtime overhead. The evaluation covers synthesized malware scenarios and 150 real-world malware samples across keyloggers, information stealers, and ransomware. The results show that RAG-guided structured prompting can support the scalable construction of malware-specific deception playbooks that remain lightweight and deterministic during runtime enforcement.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at 2026 IEEE 8th International Conference on Trust, Privacy and Security in Intelligent Systems, and Applications (TPS-ISA)</p></details> |
| **[GLM-RAG: Graph Language Models for Graph-Based Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28397v2)** | 2026-10-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) over knowledge graphs requires retrievers that can effectively capture both graph structure and semantic information. In this work, we introduce a GLM-based retriever and investigate the comparative strengths of GLM-based, GNN-based, and traditional vector-search-based retrievers in single- and multi-hop RAG settings, and with a particular focus on transferability to unseen domains. Our findings suggest that finetuned GLM retrievers generalize better out of domain, achieving SOTA on two multi-hop benchmarks. On in-domain multi-hop QA datasets they remain comparable to prior work, with promising scaling as parameters and subgraph coverage increase. GNN-based retrievers achieve higher graph coverage with an efficient training setup, whereas the vector-search baseline excels at single-hop datasets.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to AACL-IJCNLP 2026, 9 pages, 19 figures</p></details> |
| **[RAG-Stress: Probing the Limits of Evidence Reliance in Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11183v1)** | 2026-10-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Following retrieved evidence does not guarantee factual correctness: misleading evidence can induce a model to replace an answer it previously gave correctly. The protocol holds the question and reference answer fixed, edits one assertion to support a designated incorrect answer, and crosses two source priority policies with three positions of the answer span within the evidence text. We measure misleading rate (MR) on each model's subset of questions answered correctly without retrieval, alongside clean accuracy on the full evaluation set. We evaluate fifteen systems spanning API models, open models, and search agents trained with reinforcement learning on TriviaQA-RC, HotpotQA, and SearchQA, with additional English and Chinese MedQA evaluations. Instructions that prioritize documents consistently produce higher MR than those permitting reliance on prior knowledge. Averaged over models and positions, the gap ranges from 10.9 to 13.5 percentage points across the three QA datasets. Mean MR follows End $>$ Beginning $>$ Middle under both policies, although individual models do not uniformly follow this ordering. A separate paired audit of 500 questions and two checkpoints supports increased harmful override without establishing a corresponding improvement in beneficial correction. These findings distinguish evidence adherence from factual reliability and motivate evaluating whether retrieved evidence preserves, replaces, or corrects a model's answers.</p></details> |  |
| **[Quantifying Retriever-Generator Alignment in RAG with Local Explanations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.21803v3)** | 2026-10-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems combine dense retrievers and language models to ground outputs in external documents. Our approach adapts Integrated Gradients for retriever analysis, proposes a Monte Carlo-stabilized Shapley Value approximation for generator attribution, and introduces the Weighted Alignment between Retriever and Generator (WARG) metric to measure how closely the generator's document usage aligns with retriever rankings. We show that WARG captures retriever-generator alignment better than Pearson and Spearman correlations and can serve as an indicator of RAG performance. RAG-E and WARG provide a practical framework for auditing this interaction, enabling more reliable and transparent RAG systems.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at EMNLP 2026</p></details> |
| **[EC-RAG: Event Chain Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Long Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08674v1)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Current large video-language models (LVLMs) still face challenges when dealing with long videos, mainly because frames are often processed independently, making it difficult to capture temporal dependencies across events. Although retrieval-augmented approaches have been introduced to provide additional context, most of them operate at the frame or snippet level, which limits their ability to model how events evolve over time and relate to each other. In this paper, we propose Event Chain Retrieval-Augmented Generation (EC-RAG), a training-free framework that organizes video content into an explicit event chain before question answering. Instead of retrieving isolated frames or text segments, EC-RAG first partitions the video into semantically coherent segments, represents each segment using multi-modal signals, and then links them into a structured chain that preserves temporal order and captures inter-event relationships. Given a query, the system identifies relevant events within this chain and gathers supporting evidence from the associated modalities. Our approach offers several practical advantages: (i) event-level abstraction that better reflects how video content is naturally structured, enabling more reliable localization compared to frame-level retrieval; (ii) structured multi-modal fusion that aggregates speech, text, and visual cues at the event level, allowing complementary information to be more effectively utilized during reasoning; and (iii) plug-and-play compatibility with existing LVLM backbones, requiring no additional training or reliance on proprietary models. Experiments on Video-MME, MLVU, and LongVideoBench show that this event-centric design consistently outperforms frame-level retrieval baselines, highlighting the importance of modeling temporal structure for long-video understanding.</p></details> | <details><summary>12 pa...</summary><p>12 pages, 7 figures, 7 tables, including supplementary material</p></details> |

Its literal searches returned nothing when the router's arguments did not occur verbatim in a record, for example when it transliterated Greek into Latin script or combined words that occur in a record but not as one phrase, and the agent then abstained. | **[SwarmEvolve: Latent Agent Swarms for Evolutionary Discovery and Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08483v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM-guided evolutionary search uses AI agents to improve programs for mathematical and computational problems by iteratively refining solutions and combining high-scoring methods. However, the process is often slow because the LLM generates a long textual chain of thought for each round and must repeat this generation many times to maintain population diversity. We present SwarmEvolve, a framework in which hundreds of low-cost agents per round work in swarms, reason in latent space without decoding to text, and share information by passing KV caches. Each swarm studies the problem from a different angle. A novelty filter keeps the most distinct agent ideas by directly measuring the distances between the agents' hidden state vectors. Crossover of ideas happens naturally in latent space, as a summarizer agent receives the combined KV caches of all swarm members. On 21 problems in mathematical discovery and scientific computing, SwarmEvolve gets the highest average score and needs a median of only 3 to 8 minutes to reach the best scores that other state-of-the-art frameworks reach in 60 minutes. On several problems, SwarmEvolve with Qwen3 8B improves on the solutions that AlphaEvolve first found with frontier LLMs. Our work shows that agent swarms that communicate in latent space offer a fast, low-cost approach to program search for scientific problems.</p></details> |  |
| **[Multi-Agent Home Energy Management Assistant](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.15219v3)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Existing home energy management systems conceptualize occupants as passive recipients of energy information and control, which limits their ability to effectively support informed decision-making and sustained engagement. This paper presents Home Energy Management Assistant (HEMA), the first open-source, multi-agent system enabling sustained human-AI collaboration - multi-turn conversational interactions with preserved context - across diverse home energy management (HEM) tasks - from energy analysis and educational support to smart device control. HEMA combines large language model (LLM) reasoning capabilities with 36 purpose-built domain-specific tools through a three-layer architecture: a web-based conversational interface, a backend API server, and a multi-agent system. The system features three specialized agents - Analysis (energy consumption patterns and cost optimization), Knowledge (educational queries and rebate information), and Control (smart device management and scheduling) - coordinated through a self-consistency classifier that routes user queries using chain-of-thought reasoning. This architecture enables various energy analyses, adaptive explanations, and streamlined device control. HEMA also includes a comprehensive evaluation framework using an LLM-as-simulated-user methodology with 23 objective metrics across task performance, factual accuracy, interaction quality, and system efficiency, allowing systematic testing across diverse scenarios and user personas without requiring extensive human subject testing. Through demonstrations using real-world household energy consumption data, we show how HEMA supports informed decision-making and active engagement in HEM, highlighting its potential as a user-friendly, adaptable tool for residential deployment and as a research platform for HEM innovation.</p></details> | <details><summary>22 pa...</summary><p>22 pages, 9 figures. Re-set in the HUBS Lab paper layout (HUBS Lab Paper No. 2026-001); content unchanged</p></details> |
| **[Screen Before You Serve: Simulation for Production Customer Experience AI Agents at 140M Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30137v2)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Customer experience (CX) agents use tools and large language models to address customer requests and guide conversational interactions with an organization's products. Synthetic customers react to agent responses and simulated tool outputs enable multi-step agentic workflows without invoking production backends. | **[ForecastCompass: Guiding Agentic Forecasting with Adaptive Factor Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30858v2)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic forecasting is important for decision-making in dynamic environments, but it remains challenging because agents must reason from incomplete, time-limited evidence and produce calibrated probabilities before outcomes are resolved. To make this contract practical, SEER uses a CAS-based dual-layer event queue: local event queues preserve per-entity causal order, while a parallel executive list exposes ready work to global worker threads. SEER further uses STPA, a preflight-guided thread-pool selection mechanism that combines short runtime traces with static workload and hardware features. We evaluate SEER on two parameterized MARL workloads: compute-intensive UAV swarm control (Aircraft) and communication-intensive wireless management (PCS). | **[SPILLWAY: Separating Target Admission from Sanitization to Defend Tool-Using Agents against Prompt Injection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07222v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tool-using agents act on email, files, and payments, so an instruction hidden in a web page or a shared document can make an agent send data or money to an attacker. The check admits a call when each target value that appears in tool output but not in the user's task was returned by a read-only tool in a typed field or is covered by a fixed delegation rule. A deterministic sanitizer removes instruction-like regions its fixed rules match before the model reads them and retains the removed spans as evidence, and a local judge may restore a refused call, never one whose target lies in the removed text. We natively localize every layer of the benchmark: customer databases are populated with culturally appropriate records, user-simulator prompts are refined iteratively through contrastive annotation by native-language experts, and tasks are updated according to the localized database and to reflect realistic tasks in the target region. Evaluation across eight agent models shows the largest drops compared to English in the Korean and Farsi retail domains, by up to 61 points in pass^4. Code and data released at https://github.com/lilt/tau-bench-lilt.</p></details> | 17 pages, 14 figures |
| **[MammoSeek: A Mammography Agent with selective zooming for grounded reports](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07188v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Radiologists read a screening mammogram by first forming a global impression of the four views and then selectively magnifying suspicious regions before assigning a BI-RADS assessment. Therefore, we present MammoSeek, a mammography agent that reproduces this reading process. After forming an impression of each breast, the agent performs selective zooming: it decides by itself which detector candidates deserve a closer look and inspects only those on full-resolution views. Guided by a BI-RADS assessment skill, it then assigns a category and generates a localized, lexicon-structured report for each breast; its assessment is combined with image-model scores in a decision layer to decide recall. On an internal (EMBED) and an external (VinDr-Mammo) dataset, \ours reaches exam-level AUCs of 0.824 and 0.818, far above other MLLMs, mammography agent and CNN classifiers. Selective zooming also outperforms both no zooming and forced zooming in localization, structured-report F1 and recall decisions at low cost.</p></details> | 5 pages, 2 figures |
| **[SEABench: Benchmarking Endogenous Misalignment In Self-Evolving Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35596v3)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Self-evolving LLM agents have gained prominence for their ability to improve after deployment by modifying their harness, including their controller instructions, memory management protocols, and reusable tools and skills, in response to user and environment feedback. To study this risk, we introduce SEABench, a benchmark for studying endogenous misalignment arising from agent self-evolution, with 48 longitudinal task sequences that span multiple evolution surfaces, task domains, and harm types in a rich personal-assistant environment. To account for the stochasticity inherent in agentic operations, we provide an adaptive trajectory discovery pipeline that probes for failures while preserving original task intent and supports causal attribution through paired non-evolving agents and attribution scores. Our evaluation across multiple recent LLMs, evolution surfaces, and harm types reveals that self-evolution indeed increases task completion rates but often at the cost of safety failures that are absent for paired non-evolving baseline agents. We also show that qualitatively different safety behaviors emerge across evolution surfaces and harm types. Further, we show that this divergence in safety behavior is reflected in agents' chain-of-thought reasoning, which yields an effective monitoring strategy that can mitigate unsafe behavior with a low false positive rate.</p></details> |  |
| **[Position: Agentic Evolution is the Path to Evolving LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.00359v3)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As Large Language Models (LLMs) move from curated training sets into open-ended real-world environments, a fundamental limitation emerges: static training cannot keep pace with continual deployment environment change. Scaling training-time and inference-time compute improves static capability but does not close this train-deploy gap. We argue that addressing this limitation requires a new scaling axis-evolution. Existing deployment-time adaptation methods, whether parametric fine-tuning or heuristic memory accumulation, lack the strategic agency needed to diagnose failures and produce durable improvements. Our position is that agentic evolution represents the inevitable future of LLM adaptation, elevating evolution itself from a fixed pipeline to an autonomous evolver agent. We instantiate this vision in a general framework, A-Evolve, which treats deployment-time improvement as a deliberate, goal-directed optimization process over persistent system state. We further propose the evolution-scaling hypothesis: the capacity for adaptation scales with the compute allocated to evolution, positioning agentic evolution as a scalable path toward sustained, open-ended adaptation in the real world. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/A-EVO-Lab/a-evolve.</p></details> |  |
| **[Multi-step SWE: Can Coding Agents Keep Building on Their Own Code Over Long Horizons?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07166v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Developers rarely give a coding agent a single request: they ask it to build a component and then to extend, upgrade, and optimize it, each time on code the agent itself wrote. Most benchmarks, however, score one task at a time. We introduce Multi-step SWE, a long-horizon benchmark of 69 software engineering tasks, each a chain of 5-15 dependent steps on a single codebase. Every step is a complete, multi-hour engineering request that starts from the code the agent produced in the preceding steps, so early design decisions and errors carry forward. The tasks span four families of engineering work and nine languages, include both from-scratch builds and existing open-source codebases, and ground every step in an existing source of truth that defines both the instruction and its verifier. Across nine frontier models, the strongest model reaches a solve rate of only 18.5%, and 38 of the 69 tasks are not solved by any model. We release Multi-step SWE publicly at https://multistepswe.com.</p></details> | <details><summary>Prepr...</summary><p>Preprint. Code and benchmark: https://multistepswe.com/</p></details> |
| **[More Agents, More Reliable? System-Level Reliability of Multi-Agent LLMs under Operational-Profile Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07140v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Teams increasingly build software from cooperating large language model (LLM) agents, often assuming more agents mean more reliability. Agent failure-probability intervals propagate through this network, where the combining rule can itself fail, into a reliability envelope showing which evidence limits it. In a pipeline extracting regulated chemical-hazard statements, majority voting over three models protected against the weakest but did not clearly exceed the best, and a voter failing on 5\% of demands would largely erase its advantage over the middle model. More agents yield a more defensible reliability claim, not automatically a more reliable system.</p></details> | <details><summary>Submi...</summary><p>Submitted for IEEE Software Magazine</p></details> |
| **[Teaching Agents the "Principle", Not the "Reward"](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07130v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning has achieved strong performance in many tasks by letting agents learn from nothing more than a reward signal. To address this burden, we propose prescriptive policy learning (PPL), which directly teaches agents higher-level knowledge about the tasks, termed principles of behavior, such as how to maintain balance, how to accumulate energy, and how to land smoothly. Through appropriately selected key quantities and references, PPL can directly indicate at every step whether the agent follows the principles and to what extent. Our experiments show that agents can learn good, generalizable policies without any reward. Taught only simple heuristic principles, PPL agents outperform reward-based methods with far fewer samples. Their policies also transfer directly to harder settings and keep performing well, demonstrating their strong generalization ability. In summary, PPL provides a new approach in which rough knowledge of the desired behavior is enough for agents to learn good, generalizable policies, even when the system dynamics cannot be accurately modeled.</p></details> | <details><summary>15 pa...</summary><p>15 pages, 9 figures, 5 tables</p></details> |

## SFT

| **[The Alignment Tax Is Graded: Task-Selective Capability Loss Under Length-Normalized DPO with SFT Retention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06476v2)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Setting. This is an empirical characterization study; we propose no new method and run no comparisons against alternative objectives. We fine-tune Qwen3.5-4B with sparse LoRA and length-normalized DPO plus an SFT retention term (the RPO objective) on structured financial-filing tasks, and track downstream capability as the implicit reward margin grows to $\sim 7$ $β$-scaled units (nf4 training; $\sim 3$ under fp16 measurement on the unquantized base). Question. Does margin growth degrade capability uniformly across tasks, or selectively? Findings. In a pre-registered study (8 seeds, 6 unseen at freeze) with artifact-aware scoring and TOST equivalence testing, the alignment tax is graded and task-selective: extraction is preserved ($Δ= -0.55$ pts, equivalent at $\pm5$ pts), json loses little ($Δ= -3.52$ pts, equivalence fails on a clustered item-level interval), and router degrades severely ($Δ= -30$ pts). Router's loss concentrates in a margin 1-3 damage window containing a transient format-break spike. The margin is accounted for almost entirely ($\geq 96\%$) by suppression of rejected completions, with chosen reward approximately flat, consistent with the retention term anchoring it; we do not attribute the behavioral damage to any single gradient channel. Gold-logp deflation is task-selective (extraction $-0.15$, json $-1.04$, router $-1.47$ nats/token under fp16), with gold selectively protected relative to incorrect controls on extraction (8/8 seeds). QLoRA reward margins differ up to $3.4\times$ between nf4 training and unquantized fp16 measurement, with an axis-dependent gap. Scope. Length-normalized rewards with SFT retention, sparse LoRA on 8/32 layers, one 4B model, easily-separable synthetic preferences, three structured tasks. A no-retention control and a measured format-alignment metric are left to future work.</p></details> | <details><summary>25 pa...</summary><p>25 pages, 3 figures, 23 tables. Code: https://github.com/yuan-phd/delta-filing</p></details> |
| **[Unifying and Beyond the Theory of Pervasive Capability Degradation in LLM Post-training SFT from Feature Interference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05003v2)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Pervasive Capability Degradation (PCD) refers to the phenomenon in which supervised fine-tuning (SFT), while adapting a large language model (LLM) to a target task, often erodes the general capabilities acquired during pre-training. Prevailing explanations attribute PCD to macroscopic distribution shift and therefore lack microscopic manipulability, leaving existing mitigation methods without a complete theoretical foundation. In this paper, we first develop a bottom-up geometric theory of PCD grounded in feature interference under superposition: narrow-domain SFT data disrupt the almost-orthogonal feature structure formed during pre-training, and the resulting interference drives activations across decision hyperplanes in the output Laguerre-Voronoi geometry, flipping predictions on general tasks. This theory unifies existing methods into six paradigms and reveals both the source of their efficacy and their limitations. We then argue that cross-entropy under one-hot supervision is structurally limited in resolving PCD on its own, and accordingly propose the AEGIS loss, which directly constrains the drift of activation margins along all competing token directions, thereby decoupling interference suppression from task adaptation. Across 3 model families and 14 SFT tasks, AEGIS outperforms representative methods of every paradigm on target tasks and pervasive capabilities simultaneously, at only about 1.3 times the computational cost of standard SFT. Its generality, scalability, and mechanistic interpretability offer a new perspective on mitigating PCD.</p></details> | <details><summary>20 pa...</summary><p>20 pages, a very long paper :)</p></details> |
| **[From Weeks to Hours: Fast and Principled SFT Data Generation for Reasoning LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00554v1)** | 2026-10-31 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reasoning-oriented post-training enables large language models (LLMs) to solve complex tasks via multi-step inference. Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on high-quality reasoning traces is a particularly efficient approach, but its effectiveness depends critically on careful data curation. Existing pipelines rely on a costly generate-then-filter paradigm: large pools of long reasoning traces are produced by multiple teacher models and subsequently filtered for difficulty and diversity using additional LLMs, often requiring weeks of computation. In this work, we propose SFTProbe, a fundamentally different approach that bypasses this pipeline by directly predicting the quality of reasoning data. We show that a brief LoRA-based adaptation, combined with evaluating loss on only 5% of each reasoning trace, suffices to estimate both difficulty and diversity. SFTProbe is grounded in a geometric view of post-training, where pretrained LLMs lie in a low-rank, anisotropic loss basin. By probing this basin via low-rank perturbations, we construct compact loss signatures whose clustering captures diversity, while loss at curvature inflection points provides a robust proxy for difficulty. We also provide a simple criterion for teacher selection. Empirically, our approach reduces data curation time from weeks to hours while maintaining or improving performance. Remarkably, on mathematical reasoning, 5k generated examples match the highly filtered 89k-example in OpenThoughts-Math dataset, and 7.5k generated examples outperforms it by 2.5% in accuracy. We further introduce a high-quality physics reasoning dataset, and use it to train a model that outperforms an IPhO 2025 silver-medal-winning model. The code and the dataset are released at https://bigml-cs-ucla.github.io/SFTProbe-page/.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[mSFT: Addressing Dataset Mixtures Overfitting Heterogeneously in Multi-task SFT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.21606v7)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Current language model training commonly applies multi-task Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) using a homogeneous compute budget across all sub-datasets.
